People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Uh, yeah… I’m here because I just can’t seem to stop going back over things at home. Like the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust on things—I keep checking them or fixing them over and over. I’ll think I’m done, and then I’m right back at it.

It takes up most of the day sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out. My dad used to say, “measure twice, cut once,” you know, and I guess I took that way too far with tools and everything around the house. But it’s not like I feel better after checking. I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.

Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just the checking. Even when I know I did something right, like with an outlet or a little repair, this thought just jumps in—like, what if I missed a loose wire and the whole house burns down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt.

Yeah… I mean, it’s not like I have a nice neat rule written down, but I do keep going back the same way. Yeah, I think that’s the closest way to say it. I’m not sitting there thinking through some big plan every time, like “this will save someone,” but it feels like if I don’t check it again, something could go wrong.

Yeah… I guess “perfect” is kind of the word, though I don’t think of it like I’m trying to make it look nice or anything. It’s more like if I don’t get it right, I can’t let it go.

It eats up most of the day sometimes. I get worn out from it, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.

Like the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust on things—I keep checking them or fixing them over and over. I’ll think I’m done, and then I’m right back at it.\n\nIt takes up most of the day sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out. My dad used to say, “measure twice, cut once,” you know, and I guess I took that way too far with tools and everything around the house. But it’s not like I feel better after checking. I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just the checking. Even when I know I did something right, like with an outlet or a little repair, this thought just jumps in—like, what if I missed a loose wire and the whole house burns down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt.\n\nAnd I try to tell myself, no, I already looked, I already cleaned up, but then it comes back louder. It’s like when I was helping my uncle build a shed years ago, he’d always say one bad nail can ruin the whole board—sorry, that’s not really… but that kind of thing sticks in my head. I try to push it away and then it’s right back, stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or repairs in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not like I have a nice neat rule written down, but I do keep going back the same way. Like I’ll check an outlet, then the lock, then my tools, and then I notice dust or something out of place and I’m fixing that too. Then I’ll think, wait, did I really check the outlet right? So I go back again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I keep checking and fixing the same things over and over until I’m just exhausted, but even then I don’t feel settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s what it feels like underneath it, even if I don’t always say it that way. I keep going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools, even little dust or messes around the house, because I can’t shake the feeling that if I leave it, something’s going to be wrong.\n\nAnd then I check it, or fix it, and I should be done, right? But I’m not. I’ll be worn out after doing it most of the day sometimes, and still not feel sure enough to just walk away. My dad used to get on me about doing things right the first time, and I guess that kind of sticks in your head… but yeah, mostly I’m trying to stop something bad from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in the way you mean, I don’t think. Like, I can notice if something looks off, sure, like anyone would, but I’m not sitting there needing the pillows even or the cans lined up or anything like that.\n\nMy wife used to joke about how I fold towels wrong, actually—she has this whole way of doing it, and I never got it right. But no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or a certain order for me. At least I haven’t noticed that being the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing are attempts to directly prevent harm or danger to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s the closest way to say it. I’m not sitting there thinking through some big plan every time, like “this will save someone,” but it feels like if I don’t check it again, something could go wrong.\n\nThat’s why I keep going back over the outlets, the locks, the tools… even stupid little things like dust or something looking off in the house. I’ll fix it, then check it, then somehow I’m back at the outlet again. It’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you left the stove on, except it just doesn’t stop.\n\nAnd then the whole day kind of disappears. I’ll be worn out from doing it over and over, but I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing need to be perfect, or that if they're not, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “perfect” is kind of the word, though I don’t think of it like I’m trying to make it look nice or anything. It’s more like if I don’t get it right, I can’t let it go.\n\nI’ll keep going back to the same outlets, the locks, my tools, dust in the house—little stuff that probably wouldn’t bother somebody else. I check it, fix it, then I’m thinking, wait, did I really check that? And then I’m back over there again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I get worn out from it, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it mental balance exactly. Maybe it is, in a way. It’s more like I can’t get my mind to settle unless I go back over the same stuff again.

Like the outlets, the locks, my tools… and even dust around the house, which sounds ridiculous when I say it. I’ll check something, fix it, then I’ll be halfway into something else and think, wait, did I really do that right? And then I’m back there again.

It’s not like I’m trying to make the house feel peaceful or anything. I mean, my house isn’t even that organized. I’ve got a drawer in the kitchen that’s just batteries and old takeout menus and whatever else. But these certain things, I just keep circling back to them until I’m exhausted, and even then I don’t really feel sure enough to leave them alone.

I try to push it away and then it’s right back, stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or repairs in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not like I have a nice neat rule written down, but I do keep going back the same way. I keep checking and fixing the same things over and over until I’m just exhausted, but even then I don’t feel settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I think it gives me something to do with the feeling, if that makes sense. Like if my head is going in circles, then I go check the outlet, or the lock, or my tools, and for a second it feels like I’m handling it.

But then it doesn’t really stay handled. I’ll fix something, then I’m back looking at dust on a shelf or wondering if I really checked the same thing right. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it takes up most of my day sometimes. I’ll mean to do one quick check, and then hours are gone.

Kind of like when you start cleaning one little spot in the kitchen and then suddenly you’re moving the toaster and finding crumbs from who knows when… except for me it’s not really cleaning, it’s this need to keep going back over it. By the end I’m just worn out, and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. So control, maybe for a minute, but not really.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I’m here because I just can’t seem to stop going back over things at home. Like the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust on things—I keep checking them or fixing them over and over. I’ll think I’m done, and then I’m right back at it.\n\nIt takes up most of the day sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out. My dad used to say, “measure twice, cut once,” you know, and I guess I took that way too far with tools and everything around the house. But it’s not like I feel better after checking. I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just the checking. Even when I know I did something right, like with an outlet or a little repair, this thought just jumps in—like, what if I missed a loose wire and the whole house burns down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt.\n\nAnd I try to tell myself, no, I already looked, I already cleaned up, but then it comes back louder. It’s like when I was helping my uncle build a shed years ago, he’d always say one bad nail can ruin the whole board—sorry, that’s not really… but that kind of thing sticks in my head. I try to push it away and then it’s right back, stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or repairs in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not like I have a nice neat rule written down, but I do keep going back the same way. Like I’ll check an outlet, then the lock, then my tools, and then I notice dust or something out of place and I’m fixing that too. Then I’ll think, wait, did I really check the outlet right? So I go back again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I keep checking and fixing the same things over and over until I’m just exhausted, but even then I don’t feel settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s what it feels like underneath it, even if I don’t always say it that way. I keep going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools, even little dust or messes around the house, because I can’t shake the feeling that if I leave it, something’s going to be wrong.\n\nAnd then I check it, or fix it, and I should be done, right? But I’m not. I’ll be worn out after doing it most of the day sometimes, and still not feel sure enough to just walk away. My dad used to get on me about doing things right the first time, and I guess that kind of sticks in your head… but yeah, mostly I’m trying to stop something bad from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in the way you mean, I don’t think. Like, I can notice if something looks off, sure, like anyone would, but I’m not sitting there needing the pillows even or the cans lined up or anything like that.\n\nMy wife used to joke about how I fold towels wrong, actually—she has this whole way of doing it, and I never got it right. But no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or a certain order for me. At least I haven’t noticed that being the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing are attempts to directly prevent harm or danger to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s the closest way to say it. I’m not sitting there thinking through some big plan every time, like “this will save someone,” but it feels like if I don’t check it again, something could go wrong.\n\nThat’s why I keep going back over the outlets, the locks, the tools… even stupid little things like dust or something looking off in the house. I’ll fix it, then check it, then somehow I’m back at the outlet again. It’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you left the stove on, except it just doesn’t stop.\n\nAnd then the whole day kind of disappears. I’ll be worn out from doing it over and over, but I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing need to be perfect, or that if they're not, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “perfect” is kind of the word, though I don’t think of it like I’m trying to make it look nice or anything. It’s more like if I don’t get it right, I can’t let it go.\n\nI’ll keep going back to the same outlets, the locks, my tools, dust in the house—little stuff that probably wouldn’t bother somebody else. I check it, fix it, then I’m thinking, wait, did I really check that? And then I’m back over there again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I get worn out from it, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking and fixing behaviors are trying to achieve a kind of mental balance or order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mental balance exactly. Maybe it is, in a way. It’s more like I can’t get my mind to settle unless I go back over the same stuff again.\n\nLike the outlets, the locks, my tools… and even dust around the house, which sounds ridiculous when I say it. I’ll check something, fix it, then I’ll be halfway into something else and think, wait, did I really do that right? And then I’m back there again.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make the house feel peaceful or anything. I mean, my house isn’t even that organized. I’ve got a drawer in the kitchen that’s just batteries and old takeout menus and whatever else. But these certain things, I just keep circling back to them until I’m exhausted, and even then I don’t really feel sure enough to leave them alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors help you feel more in control of your thoughts or emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it gives me something to do with the feeling, if that makes sense. Like if my head is going in circles, then I go check the outlet, or the lock, or my tools, and for a second it feels like I’m handling it.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay handled. I’ll fix something, then I’m back looking at dust on a shelf or wondering if I really checked the same thing right. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it takes up most of my day sometimes. I’ll mean to do one quick check, and then hours are gone.\n\nKind of like when you start cleaning one little spot in the kitchen and then suddenly you’re moving the toaster and finding crumbs from who knows when… except for me it’s not really cleaning, it’s this need to keep going back over it. Yeah, kind of… it’s like it has to be done a certain way, or I can’t trust it.

I’ll go back over the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust in the house, and I’ll check or fix them again and again. I keep checking and fixing the same things over and over until I’m just exhausted, but even then I don’t feel settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s what it feels like underneath it, even if I don’t always say it that way. But I’m not. I’ll be worn out after doing it most of the day sometimes, and still not feel sure enough to just walk away. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really symmetrical, no… I mean, it’s not like I need the lamp lined up with the edge of the table or something. My house honestly isn’t that neat. There’s dust, there’s clutter, there’s stuff I keep meaning to deal with.

It’s more that I get stuck going back over the same things — outlets, locks, my tools, dust around the house — checking and fixing them again and again. Like I’ll look at a lock and then walk away, and then my brain says, no, go back, you didn’t really make sure. And then I’m back there.

I had this old toolbox once where the little latch never sat right, and I’d mess with it forever, even though it probably didn’t matter. Same kind of thing. It’s not about it looking pretty or matching, it’s more like I can’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I spend most of the day doing that sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out.

Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking and fixing behaviors are trying to achieve a kind of mental balance or order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mental balance exactly. Maybe it is, in a way. It’s more like I can’t get my mind to settle unless I go back over the same stuff again.\n\nLike the outlets, the locks, my tools… and even dust around the house, which sounds ridiculous when I say it. I’ll check something, fix it, then I’ll be halfway into something else and think, wait, did I really do that right? And then I’m back there again.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make the house feel peaceful or anything. I mean, my house isn’t even that organized. I’ve got a drawer in the kitchen that’s just batteries and old takeout menus and whatever else. But these certain things, I just keep circling back to them until I’m exhausted, and even then I don’t really feel sure enough to leave them alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors help you feel more in control of your thoughts or emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it gives me something to do with the feeling, if that makes sense. Like if my head is going in circles, then I go check the outlet, or the lock, or my tools, and for a second it feels like I’m handling it.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay handled. I’ll fix something, then I’m back looking at dust on a shelf or wondering if I really checked the same thing right. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it takes up most of my day sometimes. I’ll mean to do one quick check, and then hours are gone.\n\nKind of like when you start cleaning one little spot in the kitchen and then suddenly you’re moving the toaster and finding crumbs from who knows when… except for me it’s not really cleaning, it’s this need to keep going back over it. By the end I’m just worn out, and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. So control, maybe for a minute, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix need to be done in a specific, exact way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… it’s like it has to be done a certain way, or I can’t trust it.\n\nI’ll go back over the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust in the house, and I’ll check or fix them again and again. And then I’ll think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t really look close enough. It’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not like folding shirts perfectly at a store. I used to work with a guy who was really particular about stuff like that, had all his pens lined up, drove everybody nuts—but with me it’s more like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nMost of my day can get eaten up by it. I get worn out, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no… I mean, it’s not like I need the lamp lined up with the edge of the table or something. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really an order, I don’t think… like it’s not that I have to do the lock first and then the outlet and then the tools or anything like that.

It’s more I just get pulled back to the same things over and over. Outlets, locks, tools, dust in the house… I’ll check or fix one thing, then I’ll end up back at it again because it doesn’t feel settled. Sometimes I start in one room and then somehow I’m in another room looking at something else, and then I remember the first thing and go back.

It kind of reminds me of when you go to the garage to get one thing and then you notice three other things sitting there, and suddenly you forgot why you went in there. Except with me it eats up most of the day, and by the time I’m done going back over everything I’m worn out… and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.

It’s not just the checking. Even when I know I did something right, like with an outlet or a little repair, this thought just jumps in—like, what if I missed a loose wire and the whole house burns down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt.\n\nAnd I try to tell myself, no, I already looked, I already cleaned up, but then it comes back louder. It’s like when I was helping my uncle build a shed years ago, he’d always say one bad nail can ruin the whole board—sorry, that’s not really… but that kind of thing sticks in my head. I try to push it away and then it’s right back, stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or repairs in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not like I have a nice neat rule written down, but I do keep going back the same way. Like I’ll check an outlet, then the lock, then my tools, and then I notice dust or something out of place and I’m fixing that too. Then I’ll think, wait, did I really check the outlet right? So I go back again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I keep checking and fixing the same things over and over until I’m just exhausted, but even then I don’t feel settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s what it feels like underneath it, even if I don’t always say it that way. I keep going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools, even little dust or messes around the house, because I can’t shake the feeling that if I leave it, something’s going to be wrong.\n\nAnd then I check it, or fix it, and I should be done, right? But I’m not. I’ll be worn out after doing it most of the day sometimes, and still not feel sure enough to just walk away. My dad used to get on me about doing things right the first time, and I guess that kind of sticks in your head… but yeah, mostly I’m trying to stop something bad from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in the way you mean, I don’t think. Like, I can notice if something looks off, sure, like anyone would, but I’m not sitting there needing the pillows even or the cans lined up or anything like that.\n\nMy wife used to joke about how I fold towels wrong, actually—she has this whole way of doing it, and I never got it right. But no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or a certain order for me. At least I haven’t noticed that being the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing are attempts to directly prevent harm or danger to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s the closest way to say it. I’m not sitting there thinking through some big plan every time, like “this will save someone,” but it feels like if I don’t check it again, something could go wrong.\n\nThat’s why I keep going back over the outlets, the locks, the tools… even stupid little things like dust or something looking off in the house. I’ll fix it, then check it, then somehow I’m back at the outlet again. It’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you left the stove on, except it just doesn’t stop.\n\nAnd then the whole day kind of disappears. I’ll be worn out from doing it over and over, but I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing need to be perfect, or that if they're not, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “perfect” is kind of the word, though I don’t think of it like I’m trying to make it look nice or anything. It’s more like if I don’t get it right, I can’t let it go.\n\nI’ll keep going back to the same outlets, the locks, my tools, dust in the house—little stuff that probably wouldn’t bother somebody else. I check it, fix it, then I’m thinking, wait, did I really check that? And then I’m back over there again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I get worn out from it, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking and fixing behaviors are trying to achieve a kind of mental balance or order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mental balance exactly. Maybe it is, in a way. It’s more like I can’t get my mind to settle unless I go back over the same stuff again.\n\nLike the outlets, the locks, my tools… and even dust around the house, which sounds ridiculous when I say it. I’ll check something, fix it, then I’ll be halfway into something else and think, wait, did I really do that right? And then I’m back there again.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make the house feel peaceful or anything. I mean, my house isn’t even that organized. I’ve got a drawer in the kitchen that’s just batteries and old takeout menus and whatever else. But these certain things, I just keep circling back to them until I’m exhausted, and even then I don’t really feel sure enough to leave them alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors help you feel more in control of your thoughts or emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it gives me something to do with the feeling, if that makes sense. Like if my head is going in circles, then I go check the outlet, or the lock, or my tools, and for a second it feels like I’m handling it.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay handled. I’ll fix something, then I’m back looking at dust on a shelf or wondering if I really checked the same thing right. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it takes up most of my day sometimes. I’ll mean to do one quick check, and then hours are gone.\n\nKind of like when you start cleaning one little spot in the kitchen and then suddenly you’re moving the toaster and finding crumbs from who knows when… except for me it’s not really cleaning, it’s this need to keep going back over it. By the end I’m just worn out, and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. So control, maybe for a minute, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix need to be done in a specific, exact way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… it’s like it has to be done a certain way, or I can’t trust it.\n\nI’ll go back over the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust in the house, and I’ll check or fix them again and again. And then I’ll think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t really look close enough. It’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not like folding shirts perfectly at a store. I used to work with a guy who was really particular about stuff like that, had all his pens lined up, drove everybody nuts—but with me it’s more like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nMost of my day can get eaten up by it. I get worn out, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no… I mean, it’s not like I need the lamp lined up with the edge of the table or something. My house honestly isn’t that neat. There’s dust, there’s clutter, there’s stuff I keep meaning to deal with.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck going back over the same things — outlets, locks, my tools, dust around the house — checking and fixing them again and again. Like I’ll look at a lock and then walk away, and then my brain says, no, go back, you didn’t really make sure. And then I’m back there.\n\nI had this old toolbox once where the little latch never sat right, and I’d mess with it forever, even though it probably didn’t matter. Same kind of thing. It’s not about it looking pretty or matching, it’s more like I can’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I spend most of the day doing that sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be in a specific order for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really an order, I don’t think… like it’s not that I have to do the lock first and then the outlet and then the tools or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more I just get pulled back to the same things over and over. Outlets, locks, tools, dust in the house… I’ll check or fix one thing, then I’ll end up back at it again because it doesn’t feel settled. Sometimes I start in one room and then somehow I’m in another room looking at something else, and then I remember the first thing and go back.\n\nIt kind of reminds me of when you go to the garage to get one thing and then you notice three other things sitting there, and suddenly you forgot why you went in there. Except with me it eats up most of the day, and by the time I’m done going back over everything I’m worn out… and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Outlets, locks, tools, dust in the house… I’ll check or fix one thing, then I’ll end up back at it again because it doesn’t feel settled. Sometimes I start in one room and then somehow I’m in another room looking at something else, and then I remember the first thing and go back.\n\nIt kind of reminds me of when you go to the garage to get one thing and then you notice three other things sitting there, and suddenly you forgot why you went in there. Except with me it eats up most of the day, and by the time I’m done going back over everything I’m worn out… and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the things you check or fix have to be exactly right, or is it more about preventing something bad from happening?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even when I know I did the job right, these ugly thoughts keep jumping in that a loose wire I missed is going to burn the house down or a nail I left somewhere is going to hurt my wife, and I try to push them out but they come right back stronger.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more about something bad happening, I think. Like, even when part of me knows I did it right, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed a loose wire and the whole house could burn down.

Or I’ll think maybe I left a nail somewhere and my wife’s going to step on it or get hurt. And then I try to shove the thought away, like, “No, you checked it already,” but it comes back louder.

It’s funny, well not funny, but I used to do little jobs around the house and feel good about fixing things, and now it’s like every little thing turns into a whole problem in my head. So yeah… not exactly right like perfect-looking. More like I’m trying to stop some awful thing I keep picturing.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I’m here because I just can’t seem to stop going back over things at home. Like the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust on things—I keep checking them or fixing them over and over. I’ll think I’m done, and then I’m right back at it.\n\nIt takes up most of the day sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out. My dad used to say, “measure twice, cut once,” you know, and I guess I took that way too far with tools and everything around the house. But it’s not like I feel better after checking. I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just the checking. Even when I know I did something right, like with an outlet or a little repair, this thought just jumps in—like, what if I missed a loose wire and the whole house burns down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt.\n\nAnd I try to tell myself, no, I already looked, I already cleaned up, but then it comes back louder. It’s like when I was helping my uncle build a shed years ago, he’d always say one bad nail can ruin the whole board—sorry, that’s not really… but that kind of thing sticks in my head. I try to push it away and then it’s right back, stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or repairs in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not like I have a nice neat rule written down, but I do keep going back the same way. Like I’ll check an outlet, then the lock, then my tools, and then I notice dust or something out of place and I’m fixing that too. Then I’ll think, wait, did I really check the outlet right? So I go back again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I keep checking and fixing the same things over and over until I’m just exhausted, but even then I don’t feel settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s what it feels like underneath it, even if I don’t always say it that way. I keep going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools, even little dust or messes around the house, because I can’t shake the feeling that if I leave it, something’s going to be wrong.\n\nAnd then I check it, or fix it, and I should be done, right? But I’m not. I’ll be worn out after doing it most of the day sometimes, and still not feel sure enough to just walk away. My dad used to get on me about doing things right the first time, and I guess that kind of sticks in your head… but yeah, mostly I’m trying to stop something bad from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in the way you mean, I don’t think. Like, I can notice if something looks off, sure, like anyone would, but I’m not sitting there needing the pillows even or the cans lined up or anything like that.\n\nMy wife used to joke about how I fold towels wrong, actually—she has this whole way of doing it, and I never got it right. But no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or a certain order for me. At least I haven’t noticed that being the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing are attempts to directly prevent harm or danger to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s the closest way to say it. I’m not sitting there thinking through some big plan every time, like “this will save someone,” but it feels like if I don’t check it again, something could go wrong.\n\nThat’s why I keep going back over the outlets, the locks, the tools… even stupid little things like dust or something looking off in the house. I’ll fix it, then check it, then somehow I’m back at the outlet again. It’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you left the stove on, except it just doesn’t stop.\n\nAnd then the whole day kind of disappears. I’ll be worn out from doing it over and over, but I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing need to be perfect, or that if they're not, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “perfect” is kind of the word, though I don’t think of it like I’m trying to make it look nice or anything. It’s more like if I don’t get it right, I can’t let it go.\n\nI’ll keep going back to the same outlets, the locks, my tools, dust in the house—little stuff that probably wouldn’t bother somebody else. I check it, fix it, then I’m thinking, wait, did I really check that? And then I’m back over there again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I get worn out from it, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking and fixing behaviors are trying to achieve a kind of mental balance or order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mental balance exactly. Maybe it is, in a way. It’s more like I can’t get my mind to settle unless I go back over the same stuff again.\n\nLike the outlets, the locks, my tools… and even dust around the house, which sounds ridiculous when I say it. I’ll check something, fix it, then I’ll be halfway into something else and think, wait, did I really do that right? And then I’m back there again.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make the house feel peaceful or anything. I mean, my house isn’t even that organized. I’ve got a drawer in the kitchen that’s just batteries and old takeout menus and whatever else. But these certain things, I just keep circling back to them until I’m exhausted, and even then I don’t really feel sure enough to leave them alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors help you feel more in control of your thoughts or emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it gives me something to do with the feeling, if that makes sense. Like if my head is going in circles, then I go check the outlet, or the lock, or my tools, and for a second it feels like I’m handling it.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay handled. I’ll fix something, then I’m back looking at dust on a shelf or wondering if I really checked the same thing right. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it takes up most of my day sometimes. I’ll mean to do one quick check, and then hours are gone.\n\nKind of like when you start cleaning one little spot in the kitchen and then suddenly you’re moving the toaster and finding crumbs from who knows when… except for me it’s not really cleaning, it’s this need to keep going back over it. By the end I’m just worn out, and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. So control, maybe for a minute, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix need to be done in a specific, exact way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… it’s like it has to be done a certain way, or I can’t trust it.\n\nI’ll go back over the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust in the house, and I’ll check or fix them again and again. And then I’ll think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t really look close enough. It’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not like folding shirts perfectly at a store. I used to work with a guy who was really particular about stuff like that, had all his pens lined up, drove everybody nuts—but with me it’s more like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nMost of my day can get eaten up by it. I get worn out, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no… I mean, it’s not like I need the lamp lined up with the edge of the table or something. And then I try to shove the thought away, like, “No, you checked it already,” but it comes back louder.\n\nIt’s funny, well not funny, but I used to do little jobs around the house and feel good about fixing things, and now it’s like every little thing turns into a whole problem in my head. So yeah… not exactly right like perfect-looking. More like I’m trying to stop some awful thing I keep picturing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like the things you check or fix need to be done in a very precise or meticulous way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I guess I can get pretty particular with it, but not like I’m doing careful craftsmanship or something. It’s more that I can’t stop going back over it.

Like with outlets, locks, my tools, even dust around the house — I’ll check it or fix it, then I’ll walk away, and then I’m right back there doing it again. Sometimes I lose most of the day like that. It’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who could fix something and move on, you know? My dad was like that too, always had this old coffee can full of screws in the garage, and somehow he always knew which one he needed… I don’t know why I thought of that.

But yeah, with me now it’s just checking and fixing the same things again and again until I’m worn out. And even then, I still don’t really feel sure enough to leave it alone.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Uh, yeah… I’m here because I just can’t seem to stop going back over things at home. Like the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust on things—I keep checking them or fixing them over and over. I’ll think I’m done, and then I’m right back at it.\n\nIt takes up most of the day sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out. My dad used to say, “measure twice, cut once,” you know, and I guess I took that way too far with tools and everything around the house. But it’s not like I feel better after checking. I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, aside from the need to check things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. It’s not just the checking. Even when I know I did something right, like with an outlet or a little repair, this thought just jumps in—like, what if I missed a loose wire and the whole house burns down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt.\n\nAnd I try to tell myself, no, I already looked, I already cleaned up, but then it comes back louder. It’s like when I was helping my uncle build a shed years ago, he’d always say one bad nail can ruin the whole board—sorry, that’s not really… but that kind of thing sticks in my head. I try to push it away and then it’s right back, stronger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or repairs in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not like I have a nice neat rule written down, but I do keep going back the same way. Like I’ll check an outlet, then the lock, then my tools, and then I notice dust or something out of place and I’m fixing that too. Then I’ll think, wait, did I really check the outlet right? So I go back again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I keep checking and fixing the same things over and over until I’m just exhausted, but even then I don’t feel settled enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking and fixing things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s what it feels like underneath it, even if I don’t always say it that way. I keep going back over the same outlets, the locks, my tools, even little dust or messes around the house, because I can’t shake the feeling that if I leave it, something’s going to be wrong.\n\nAnd then I check it, or fix it, and I should be done, right? But I’m not. I’ll be worn out after doing it most of the day sometimes, and still not feel sure enough to just walk away. My dad used to get on me about doing things right the first time, and I guess that kind of sticks in your head… but yeah, mostly I’m trying to stop something bad from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or symmetrical for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really in the way you mean, I don’t think. Like, I can notice if something looks off, sure, like anyone would, but I’m not sitting there needing the pillows even or the cans lined up or anything like that.\n\nMy wife used to joke about how I fold towels wrong, actually—she has this whole way of doing it, and I never got it right. But no, I don’t think it’s about symmetry or a certain order for me. At least I haven’t noticed that being the thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing are attempts to directly prevent harm or danger to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s the closest way to say it. I’m not sitting there thinking through some big plan every time, like “this will save someone,” but it feels like if I don’t check it again, something could go wrong.\n\nThat’s why I keep going back over the outlets, the locks, the tools… even stupid little things like dust or something looking off in the house. I’ll fix it, then check it, then somehow I’m back at the outlet again. It’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you left the stove on, except it just doesn’t stop.\n\nAnd then the whole day kind of disappears. I’ll be worn out from doing it over and over, but I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing need to be perfect, or that if they're not, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “perfect” is kind of the word, though I don’t think of it like I’m trying to make it look nice or anything. It’s more like if I don’t get it right, I can’t let it go.\n\nI’ll keep going back to the same outlets, the locks, my tools, dust in the house—little stuff that probably wouldn’t bother somebody else. I check it, fix it, then I’m thinking, wait, did I really check that? And then I’m back over there again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I get worn out from it, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking and fixing behaviors are trying to achieve a kind of mental balance or order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mental balance exactly. Maybe it is, in a way. It’s more like I can’t get my mind to settle unless I go back over the same stuff again.\n\nLike the outlets, the locks, my tools… and even dust around the house, which sounds ridiculous when I say it. I’ll check something, fix it, then I’ll be halfway into something else and think, wait, did I really do that right? And then I’m back there again.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make the house feel peaceful or anything. I mean, my house isn’t even that organized. I’ve got a drawer in the kitchen that’s just batteries and old takeout menus and whatever else. But these certain things, I just keep circling back to them until I’m exhausted, and even then I don’t really feel sure enough to leave them alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors help you feel more in control of your thoughts or emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it gives me something to do with the feeling, if that makes sense. Like if my head is going in circles, then I go check the outlet, or the lock, or my tools, and for a second it feels like I’m handling it.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay handled. I’ll fix something, then I’m back looking at dust on a shelf or wondering if I really checked the same thing right. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it takes up most of my day sometimes. I’ll mean to do one quick check, and then hours are gone.\n\nKind of like when you start cleaning one little spot in the kitchen and then suddenly you’re moving the toaster and finding crumbs from who knows when… except for me it’s not really cleaning, it’s this need to keep going back over it. By the end I’m just worn out, and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. So control, maybe for a minute, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix need to be done in a specific, exact way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… it’s like it has to be done a certain way, or I can’t trust it.\n\nI’ll go back over the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust in the house, and I’ll check or fix them again and again. And then I’ll think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t really look close enough. It’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not like folding shirts perfectly at a store. I used to work with a guy who was really particular about stuff like that, had all his pens lined up, drove everybody nuts—but with me it’s more like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nMost of my day can get eaten up by it. I get worn out, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no… I mean, it’s not like I need the lamp lined up with the edge of the table or something. My house honestly isn’t that neat. There’s dust, there’s clutter, there’s stuff I keep meaning to deal with.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck going back over the same things — outlets, locks, my tools, dust around the house — checking and fixing them again and again. Like I’ll look at a lock and then walk away, and then my brain says, no, go back, you didn’t really make sure. And then I’m back there.\n\nI had this old toolbox once where the little latch never sat right, and I’d mess with it forever, even though it probably didn’t matter. Same kind of thing. It’s not about it looking pretty or matching, it’s more like I can’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I spend most of the day doing that sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be in a specific order for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really an order, I don’t think… like it’s not that I have to do the lock first and then the outlet and then the tools or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more I just get pulled back to the same things over and over. I’ll fix it, then check it, then somehow I’m back at the outlet again. It’s like when you leave the house and wonder if you left the stove on, except it just doesn’t stop.\n\nAnd then the whole day kind of disappears. I’ll be worn out from doing it over and over, but I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing need to be perfect, or that if they're not, something bad will happen?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess “perfect” is kind of the word, though I don’t think of it like I’m trying to make it look nice or anything. It’s more like if I don’t get it right, I can’t let it go.\n\nI’ll keep going back to the same outlets, the locks, my tools, dust in the house—little stuff that probably wouldn’t bother somebody else. I check it, fix it, then I’m thinking, wait, did I really check that? And then I’m back over there again.\n\nIt eats up most of the day sometimes. I get worn out from it, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. Like if I don’t do it right, something bad could happen… even if I know that probably sounds over the top.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking and fixing behaviors are trying to achieve a kind of mental balance or order?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it mental balance exactly. Maybe it is, in a way. It’s more like I can’t get my mind to settle unless I go back over the same stuff again.\n\nLike the outlets, the locks, my tools… and even dust around the house, which sounds ridiculous when I say it. I’ll check something, fix it, then I’ll be halfway into something else and think, wait, did I really do that right? And then I’m back there again.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to make the house feel peaceful or anything. I mean, my house isn’t even that organized. I’ve got a drawer in the kitchen that’s just batteries and old takeout menus and whatever else. But these certain things, I just keep circling back to them until I’m exhausted, and even then I don’t really feel sure enough to leave them alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking and fixing behaviors help you feel more in control of your thoughts or emotions?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think it gives me something to do with the feeling, if that makes sense. Like if my head is going in circles, then I go check the outlet, or the lock, or my tools, and for a second it feels like I’m handling it.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay handled. I’ll fix something, then I’m back looking at dust on a shelf or wondering if I really checked the same thing right. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because it takes up most of my day sometimes. I’ll mean to do one quick check, and then hours are gone.\n\nKind of like when you start cleaning one little spot in the kitchen and then suddenly you’re moving the toaster and finding crumbs from who knows when… except for me it’s not really cleaning, it’s this need to keep going back over it. By the end I’m just worn out, and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. So control, maybe for a minute, but not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix need to be done in a specific, exact way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah, kind of… it’s like it has to be done a certain way, or I can’t trust it.\n\nI’ll go back over the outlets, the locks, my tools, even dust in the house, and I’ll check or fix them again and again. And then I’ll think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t really look close enough. It’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not like folding shirts perfectly at a store. I used to work with a guy who was really particular about stuff like that, had all his pens lined up, drove everybody nuts—but with me it’s more like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nMost of my day can get eaten up by it. I get worn out, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no… I mean, it’s not like I need the lamp lined up with the edge of the table or something. My house honestly isn’t that neat. There’s dust, there’s clutter, there’s stuff I keep meaning to deal with.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck going back over the same things — outlets, locks, my tools, dust around the house — checking and fixing them again and again. Like I’ll look at a lock and then walk away, and then my brain says, no, go back, you didn’t really make sure. And then I’m back there.\n\nI had this old toolbox once where the little latch never sat right, and I’d mess with it forever, even though it probably didn’t matter. Same kind of thing. It’s not about it looking pretty or matching, it’s more like I can’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I spend most of the day doing that sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be in a specific order for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really an order, I don’t think… like it’s not that I have to do the lock first and then the outlet and then the tools or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more I just get pulled back to the same things over and over. Outlets, locks, tools, dust in the house… I’ll check or fix one thing, then I’ll end up back at it again because it doesn’t feel settled. Sometimes I start in one room and then somehow I’m in another room looking at something else, and then I remember the first thing and go back.\n\nIt kind of reminds me of when you go to the garage to get one thing and then you notice three other things sitting there, and suddenly you forgot why you went in there. Except with me it eats up most of the day, and by the time I’m done going back over everything I’m worn out… and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be exactly right, or is it more about preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening, I think. Like, even when part of me knows I did it right, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed a loose wire and the whole house could burn down.\n\nOr I’ll think maybe I left a nail somewhere and my wife’s going to step on it or get hurt. And then I try to shove the thought away, like, “No, you checked it already,” but it comes back louder.\n\nIt’s funny, well not funny, but I used to do little jobs around the house and feel good about fixing things, and now it’s like every little thing turns into a whole problem in my head. So yeah… not exactly right like perfect-looking. More like I’m trying to stop some awful thing I keep picturing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix need to be done in a very precise or meticulous way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I can get pretty particular with it, but not like I’m doing careful craftsmanship or something. It’s more that I can’t stop going back over it.\n\nLike with outlets, locks, my tools, even dust around the house — I’ll check it or fix it, then I’ll walk away, and then I’m right back there doing it again. Sometimes I lose most of the day like that. It’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who could fix something and move on, you know? My dad was like that too, always had this old coffee can full of screws in the garage, and somehow he always knew which one he needed… I don’t know why I thought of that.\n\nBut yeah, with me now it’s just checking and fixing the same things again and again until I’m worn out. And even then, I still don’t really feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I think… maybe I’d stop if I could actually feel sure, but that’s the problem. I don’t really get to that point.

I’ll check the outlet, or the lock, or put the tools back, or wipe at dust, and for a second I think, okay, that’s done. Then I’m walking away and it’s like my mind goes, “Are you sure though?” And then I’m back over there again.

It ends up taking most of the day sometimes, just going over the same things. It’s like when you keep patting your pockets for your keys even though you know they’re there, except it’s outlets and locks and stuff around the house, and it’s all day. By the end I’m worn out, but I still don’t feel settled enough to just leave it alone.

And then I’ll think, no, that wasn’t right, or I didn’t really look close enough. It’s not like… I don’t know, it’s not like folding shirts perfectly at a store. I used to work with a guy who was really particular about stuff like that, had all his pens lined up, drove everybody nuts—but with me it’s more like I can’t leave it alone.\n\nMost of my day can get eaten up by it. I get worn out, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be symmetrical or look a certain way for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no… I mean, it’s not like I need the lamp lined up with the edge of the table or something. My house honestly isn’t that neat. There’s dust, there’s clutter, there’s stuff I keep meaning to deal with.\n\nIt’s more that I get stuck going back over the same things — outlets, locks, my tools, dust around the house — checking and fixing them again and again. Like I’ll look at a lock and then walk away, and then my brain says, no, go back, you didn’t really make sure. And then I’m back there.\n\nI had this old toolbox once where the little latch never sat right, and I’d mess with it forever, even though it probably didn’t matter. Same kind of thing. It’s not about it looking pretty or matching, it’s more like I can’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I spend most of the day doing that sometimes, and by the end I’m just worn out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be in a specific order for you to feel okay about them?\n\nPatient: Not really an order, I don’t think… like it’s not that I have to do the lock first and then the outlet and then the tools or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more I just get pulled back to the same things over and over. Outlets, locks, tools, dust in the house… I’ll check or fix one thing, then I’ll end up back at it again because it doesn’t feel settled. Sometimes I start in one room and then somehow I’m in another room looking at something else, and then I remember the first thing and go back.\n\nIt kind of reminds me of when you go to the garage to get one thing and then you notice three other things sitting there, and suddenly you forgot why you went in there. Except with me it eats up most of the day, and by the time I’m done going back over everything I’m worn out… and I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or fix have to be exactly right, or is it more about preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening, I think. And then I try to shove the thought away, like, “No, you checked it already,” but it comes back louder.\n\nIt’s funny, well not funny, but I used to do little jobs around the house and feel good about fixing things, and now it’s like every little thing turns into a whole problem in my head. My dad was like that too, always had this old coffee can full of screws in the garage, and somehow he always knew which one he needed… I don’t know why I thought of that.\n\nBut yeah, with me now it’s just checking and fixing the same things again and again until I’m worn out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… arranged like lined up a certain way? I haven’t really noticed that being the thing, exactly.

I mean, sometimes I move stuff around while I’m messing with it, but I can’t tell if it’s because it has to look a certain way or just because I’m already there looking at it too long. Like in the garage, there’s this shelf where everything ends up kind of… well, it’s a mess, honestly. My wife says I have three different places for the same kind of thing, which is probably true. I’ll find a screwdriver in a drawer and then another one in an old paint tray, and then I start thinking about how I should build a better shelf, but then that turns into its own whole project.

So, I guess no, not really a specific arrangement. Or at least that’s not what I’m aware of. It’s more… I’m not sure. I’d have to think about what you mean by organized.
